The Hochschild Homology of Reedy Categories
Alex Ballow Montana State University

Abstract
. We calculate the Hochschild homology of generalized Reedy categories, such as the simplex category, the category of finite sets, the category of finite-dimensional categories, and the PROP associated to an operad.



Introduction

In this paper we aim to calculate a version of Hochschild homology of generalized Reedy categories. Hochschild homology traditionally is evaluated on associative algebras over rings. However Hochschild homology can be calculated using just the bar construction and tensor product, both of which have an analog in general monoidal categories. This process, described more explicitly in
Section

1.2
, gives a generalization of Hochschild homology which can be calculated for any arbitrary category. In this generality calculating Hochschild homology is not a tractable problem, so we focus on calculating it for generalized Reedy categories. Generalized Reedy categories are filtered categories where every morphism has a factorization.

The category of finite sets,
\Fin
, is a central example of generalized Reedy categories. The filtration is given by cardinality and morphism factor around the image. This example is especially motivating for our calculation as Hochschild homology approximates K-theory and the K-theory of
\Fin
is known to be the sphere spectrum
[
BP72
]
. We explicitly calculate the Hochschild homology for this and a few other generalized Reedy categories in corollaries for ease of reference. The Reedy structure for most of our chosen examples is well known, but we also include a less common example of a Reedy category: the PROP associated to an operad in
Sets
. The specific examples will be calculated using the more general result from the following theorem.

{restatable*}

theoremtest

Given a generalized Reedy category,
\mathcal{R}
, there is an equivalence between spaces:

{\sf HH}(\mathcal{R})\simeq\coprod_{n\geq 0}\coprod_{[r]\in\mathcal{R}_{=n}}\coprod_{[\phi]\in\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r)_{/^{\sf conj}}}\mathsf{B}\mathsf{Z}_{\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r)}(\phi)

where
\mathsf{Z}_{\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r)}(\phi)
is the centralizer of
\phi
in
\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r)
and
[r]\in\mathcal{R}_{=n}
denotes a choice of representative
r
for each isomorphism class of objects in
\mathcal{R}_{=n}
.

As an immediate corollary, we calculate the Hochschild homology for the common Reedy categories listed in
Example

1.1.5
.

Corollary 0.0.1
.

An application of
Introduction

\displaystyle\hh(\Fin)\simeq\coprod\limits_{n\geq 0}\coprod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\hskip 1.55554pt\text{cycle}\\
\sf\text{type}\hskip 1.55554pt\omega\end{subarray}}\mathsf{B}\mathsf{Z}_{\Sigma_{n}}(\omega)~;

\displaystyle\hh(\finvect)\simeq\coprod\limits_{n\geq 0}\coprod\limits_{[M]\in GL_{n{/^{\sf conj}}}}\mathsf{B}\mathsf{Z}_{GL_{n}}(M)~;

\displaystyle\hh(\mathbf{\Delta})\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}~;

\displaystyle\hh(\para)\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{Z}~;

\displaystyle\hh(\Lambda)\simeq\coprod\limits_{n\geq 0}\mathbb{Z}_{/n\mathbb{Z}}~;

\displaystyle\hh(PROP({\sf Assoc}))\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

where
Assoc
is the associative operad.

The layout of the remaining content begins with an overview of necessary background material in Section 1. Specifically, it gives a review of generalized Reedy categories before discussing formulism established in
[
AMGR25
]
. The formulism develops quiver representations of
\infty
-categories and leverages the inherently cyclic nature of Hochschild homology. In Section 2 we prove the theorem through a series of inductive arguments. The proof begins with induction on the degree of the Reedy structure. Within that inductive step we induct again, this time over the filtration of
\para
. The second inductive argument constructs a map with disjoint image such that the first inductive hypothesis applies to a piece. The map is homotopic to the identity, so the image is homotopy equivalent to the domain. This proves the inductive step of the first induction, finishing the proof.

0.1.

1.
Definitions, Notation, and Preliminary Results

1.1.
Reedy Categories

We begin with some facts about generalized Reedy categories.

Definition 1.1.1
.

A
generalized Reedy category
, or just
Reedy category
, is a category
\mathcal{R}
together with two subcategories
\mathcal{R}^{-}
and
\mathcal{R}^{+}
and a function
\deg:\Obj(\mathcal{R})\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
called degree satisfying the following:

(1)

given a morphism
f:X\to Y
in
\mathcal{R}^{+}
, then
\deg(X)\leq\deg(Y)
i.e. it raises degree;

(2)

given a morphism
f:X\to Y
in
\mathcal{R}^{-}
, then
\deg(X)\geq\deg(Y)
i.e. it lowers degree;

(3)

given a morphism
f:X\to Y
, then
f
is an isomorphism if and only if
f\in\mathcal{R}^{+}\cap\mathcal{R}^{-}
i.e. isomorphisms preserve degree.

(4)

every morphism
f
in
\mathcal{R}
factors uniquely up to unique isomorphism as a morphism
f^{-}
in
\mathcal{R}^{-}
followed by a morphism
f^{+}\in\mathcal{R}^{+}
.

The full subcategory consisting of those objects with degree less than (greater than) or equal to
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
will be denoted
\mathcal{R}_{\leq n}
(
\mathcal{R}_{\geq n}
). The subcategory generated by objects with degree
n
and degree preserving morphisms will be denoted
\mathcal{R}_{=n}
. Finally the intermediary object in a factorization of
f
will be denoted
{\sf im}(f)
.
Item

4
above then implies that for every
f:X\to Y
in
\mathcal{R}
we have the following communitive diagram:

Remark 1.1.2
.

There is some variance in what exactly the definition of a Reedy category should be. In deciding which definition to use we prioritize future generalization. Namely, we nearly use the generalized Reedy category definition used in
[
BM10
]
(Definition 1.1). We drop their condition (iv), which required certain isomorphisms to be the identity. This justifies our use of
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
rather than an arbitrary ordinal. We drop this condition to enable
Remark

1.1.3
.

Remark 1.1.3
.

The above definition of a Reedy category manifestly affords an extension to
\infty
-categories. We believe the results presented here are valid in that generalization.

Observation 1.1.4
.

It follows from
Definition

1.1.1
(
3
) that
\mathcal{R}^{+}
and
\mathcal{R}^{-}
each contain all of the objects of
\mathcal{R}
, i.e. they are wide subcategories.

Before we proceed let us introduce a few examples of Reedy categories. We will explicitly calculate the factorization homology of all of the following.

Example 1.1.5
.

The following are all Reedy categories:

(1)

\Fin
- The category of finite sets where the degree is given by cardinality,
\Fin^{+}
consists of surjections,
\Fin^{-}
consists of injections and the factorization required by
Item

4
is the factorization of set maps through their image;

(2)

\finvect
- The category of finite vector spaces over a field, with degree given by dimension and the factorization of a morphism
f
is again given by a surjection into the image of
f
and an injection into the codomain;

(3)

\mathbf{\Delta}
- The simplex category where degree is given by
[p]\mapsto p
and the factorization is again surjections and injections through the image;

(4)

\para
- The paracyclic category (
Definition

1.2.3
) where the degree is given by the cardinality of
[i,1\cdot i]
and the factorization is again through the image;

(5)

\Lambda
- Connes cyclic category as defined in
[
Con83
]
where the degree is given by the cardinality of the cyclically ordered finite set and the factorization is through the image;

(6)

The PROP associated to an operad in
Sets
.

The Reedy structure was named for all the examples except
Example

1.1.5
(
6
), which we will discuss more thoroughly. Intuitively, an operad is a method to describe algebraic structure, or
n
-ary operations, in a monoidal category. PROPs generalize this structure to include the possibility of operations with multiple outputs.

Observation 1.1.6
.

Given an operad
\mathcal{O}
in
\Sets
,
PROP(\mathcal{O})
is a generalized Reedy category with
PROP(\mathcal{O})^{-}
(
PROP(\mathcal{O})^{+}
) the subcategory whose morphisms are carried to decreasing (increasing) morphisms in
\Fin
. These subcategories do form a factorization system since
\mathcal{O}
is unital.

Remark 1.1.7
.

The morphisms in
PROP(\mathcal{O})
can be depicted as trees, with the inputs as the leftmost nodes and the outputs as the rightmost nodes. Composition in this depiction corresponds to concatenation. For example here is the tree representing a morphism
5\to 3
, specifically of
(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})\in\mathcal{O}(3)\times\mathcal{O}(0)\times\mathcal{O}(2)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{f_{2}}}}}{{\bullet}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3608,0.8392}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.3608,0.3608,0.8392}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}

This makes the Reedy category structure obvious. The factorization of a morphism
f
is given by first doing any non-nullary operations followed by the identity and any necessary nullary operations. As an example we factor the morphism
(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})\in\mathcal{O}(3)\times\mathcal{O}(0)\times\mathcal{O}(2)
from above.

{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -96.08 -79.5 L -14.89 -45.75}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}~.

Explicitly, this picture is denoting the composition (as in the definition of operad) of
(f_{1},f_{3})\in\mathcal{O}(3)\times\mathcal{O}(2)
with
(\id,f_{2},\id)\in\mathcal{O}(1)\times\mathcal{O}(0)\times\mathcal{O}(1)
.
Note that this analysis relies on
\mathcal{O}(1)
being a discrete category which is true of operads over sets. We believe the results in this paper apply to PROPs of operads over more general categories (i.e.
\spaces
), but we omit any such results here.

Now we get back on track with some useful properties of Reedy categories.

Observation 1.1.8
.

The opposite of a Reedy category,
\mathcal{R}
is also a Reedy category. Namely the degree map is unchanged,
(\mathcal{R}^{\sf op})^{+}=\mathcal{R}^{-}
, and
(\mathcal{R}^{\sf op})^{-}=\mathcal{R}^{+}
.

Lemma 1.1.9
.

Given morphisms
A\xrightarrow{f}B\xrightarrow{g}C
in a Reedy category,
\mathcal{R}
, such that
\deg(B)<n
, then
\deg(\im(f)),\deg(\im(g))<n
.

Proof.

We begin by factoring both
f
and
g
:

A\xrightarrow{f^{-}}\im(f)\xrightarrow{f^{+}}B\xrightarrow{g^{-}}\im(g)\xrightarrow{g^{+}}C~.

Now as
\deg(B)<n
and
\im(f)\xrightarrow{f^{+}}B
is an increasing morphism then
\im(f)
must have had degree less than
n
. Similarly, as
B\xrightarrow{g^{-}}\im(g)
is decreasing,
\deg(\im(g))<n
.
∎

Lemma 1.1.10
.

The factorization in
Definition

1.1.1
(
4
) is functorial. In other words, given factorizations of
f:X\to X^{\prime}
and
g:Y\to Y^{\prime}
along with functors
\eta:X\to Y
and
\eta^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}
such that their square commutes then the dotted arrow exists and makes the following diagram commute

Proof.

and factor the horizontal morphisms

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}~.

Since the factorization system is unique up to isomorphism, there exists an isomorphism

\phi:\im(\eta^{\prime}\circ f^{+})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\im(g^{-}\circ\eta)

making the diagram commute. Then there is a morphism

(g^{-}\circ\eta)^{+}\circ\phi\circ(\eta^{\prime}\circ f^{+})^{-}:\im(f)\to\im(g)

as desired.
∎

Lemma 1.1.11
.

Let
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\to{\sf Cat}
such that
N\mapsto\mathcal{C}_{N}
be a functor. Then the canonical maps
\Obj(\underset{N\to\infty}{\colim}\mathcal{C}_{N})\leftarrow\underset{N\to\infty}{\colim}\Obj(\mathcal{C}_{N})
and
\mathsf{Mor}(\underset{N\to\infty}{\colim}\mathcal{C}_{N})\leftarrow\underset{N\to\infty}{\colim}\mathsf{Mor}(\mathcal{C}_{N})
are equivalences.

Proof.

Recall the fully faithful functor
{\sf Cat}\xhookrightarrow{N}\Psh(\mathbf{\Delta})
from Rezk
[
Rez01
]
with image consisting of the complete Segal spaces. The Segal condition and the completeness conditions on a simplicial space each stipulate preservation of finite limit diagrams. Therefore by the definition of filtered categories, a filtered colimit of complete Segal simplicial spaces is again a complete Segal simplicial space. In other words, the fully faithful functor
{\sf Cat}\xhookrightarrow{N}\Psh(\mathbf{\Delta})
preserves filtered colimits. Since colimits in a presheaf category are computed point-wise, the desired result follows from the identities
N\mathcal{C}[0]=\Obj(\mathcal{C})
and
N\mathcal{C}[1]=\Mor(\mathcal{C})
.
∎

This is immediately applicable to Reedy categories.

Observation 1.1.12
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a Reedy category. The canonical functor
\underset{N\to\infty}{\colim}\mathcal{R}_{\leq N}\to\mathcal{R}
is an equivalence. Indeed,
Lemma

1.1.11
applied to objects implies this functor is essentially surjective and
Lemma

1.1.11
applied to morphisms gives full and faithfulness.

1.2.
Hochschild Homology

First recall that the conventional definition of Hochschild homology is the homology of the Hochschild chain complex defined below.

Definition 1.2.1
.

Given a ring
A
, its
Hochschild chain complex,
{\sf HC_{\bullet}(A)}
, is defined by
{\sf HC}_{n}(A):=A^{\otimes n+1}
and the differential
b_{n}:=\Sigma^{n}_{j=0}(-1)^{j}d_{j}
. Here the maps
d_{i}:{\sf HC}_{n}(A)\to{\sf HC}_{n-1}(A)
are as follows:

d_{i}(a_{0}\otimes a_{1}\otimes...\otimes a_{n})=\begin{cases}a_{0}a_{1}\otimes a_{2}\otimes...\otimes a_{n}&i=0\\
a_{n}a_{0}\otimes a_{1}\otimes...\otimes a_{n-1}&i=n\\
a_{0}\otimes a_{1}\otimes...\otimes a_{i}a_{i+1}\otimes...\otimes a_{n}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

In order to generalize from rings to categories, it helps to look at Hochschild homology through the lens provided by the Dold-Kan correspondence
[
Kan58
]
. Recall that the Dold-Kan correspondance is an equivalence between the category of (nonnegatively graded) chain complexes and the category of simplicial abelian groups. Through this viewpoint, the chain complex
{\sf HC}_{\bullet}(A)
corresponds to a simplicial abelian group: the
cyclic bar construction
. The cyclic bar construction can then be generalized to
\infty
-categories. This perspective motivates
[
AMGR25
]
Definition 3.1.2 of the Hochschild homology of a
\infty
-category, which is a colimit of a simplicial object.

We follow the framework developed in
[
AMGR25
]
where they understand
\infty
-categories by probing them with quivers.

Definition 1.2.2
.

A
quiver
is a category which is the free category on a finite directed graph. The category
\quiv
is the full subcategory
\quiv\subset{\sf Cat}
consisting of those categories that are quivers.

The cyclic nature of Hochschild homology is well-documented, and can be used as a computational tool. This cyclicity can be realized through Definition 2.4.1 in
[
AMGR25
]
.

Definition 1.2.3
.

An object in the
paracyclic category
\para
is a nonempty linearly ordered set
I
for which, for each
i<j
in
I
the interval
[i,j]\subset I
is finite and equipped with an action by the additive group
\mathbb{Z}
for which for each
i\in I
there is a relation
i<1\cdot i
for all
i\in I
. A morphism in
\para
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-equivariant (weakly) order-preserving map. Composition is composition of maps. Identities are identity maps.

This category admits a natural functor as described in Notation 3.1.1 in
[
AMGR25
]

(1.2.1)

\displaystyle\para

\displaystyle\to\quiv

\displaystyle\lambda=(\mathbb{Z}\curvearrowright I)

\displaystyle\mapsto\overline{\lambda}:=I_{/\mathbb{Z}}

where
I_{/\mathbb{Z}}
is the cyclically directed graph of the
\mathbb{Z}
quotient. For example if the interval
[i,j]
is given by
\{i=:i_{1},...,i_{k}:=1\cdot i\}
then
I_{/\mathbb{Z}}
looks like

\hbox to120.13pt{\vbox to82.25pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 60.06483pt\lower-41.1221pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

Additionally we have the functor

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Delta}

\displaystyle\to\para

(1.2.2)

\displaystyle[p]

\displaystyle\mapsto\chi_{p}:=[p]^{\star\mathbb{Z}}~.

where
\star
is the join of posets, so
[p]^{\star\mathbb{Z}}
is the
\mathbb{Z}
-fold join of
[p]
.
Composing,
Eq.

1.2.1
and
Eq.

1.2.2
, gives rise to a new functor,

\chi:\mathbf{\Delta}\to\para\to\quiv~.

Definition 1.2.4
.

(
[
AMGR25
]
Definition 4.1.1) The
Hochschild homology of a category
\mathcal{C}
,
\hh(\mathcal{C})
is the space

\hh(\mathcal{C}):=\colim\left(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\sf op}\xrightarrow{\chi}{\sf Quiv}^{\sf op}\xrightarrow{\hom_{\sf Cat}(\_,\mathcal{C})}\Gpd\xrightarrow{|\_|}\spaces\right)

where
|\_|
is the geometric realization functor.

Towards working with this definition, recall the unstraightening construction functor
\Un:{\sf Fun}(\mathcal{C},\Spaces)\longrightarrow{\sf Cat}_{(\infty,1)/\mathcal{C}}
and
[
Lur08
]
Corollary 3.3.4.6.

Lemma 1.2.5
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category and
F\in{\sf Fun}(\mathcal{C},\Spaces)
be a functor into
\spaces
. Then there is a canonical equivalence between spaces
\colim(F)\simeq|\Un F|
.

We will not discuss unstraightening in full generality. We will quickly review the simpler Grothendieck construction. The unstraightening is an
\infty
-categorical version of the Grothendieck construction for functors from an ordinary category to
\Gpd
. For a more comprehensive treatment of unstraightening see chapter 3 section 2 in
[
Lur08
]
.

Definition 1.2.6
.

Let
F:\mathcal{D}\to\Gpd
be a functor from any category to the groupoids of categories. The
Grothendieck construction
for
F
is the category
\Un(F)
in which an object is a pair
(d,x)
, with
d\in\Obj(\mathcal{D})
and
x\in\Obj(F(d))
; and in which a morphism from
(d_{1},x_{1})
to
(d_{2},x_{2}))
is a pair
(f,g)
where
f:d_{1}\to d_{2}
in
\mathcal{D}
, and
g:F(f)(x_{1})\to x_{2}
in
F(d_{2})
. Composition of morphisms is defined as
(f,g)\circ(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})=(f\circ f^{\prime},g\circ F(f)(g^{\prime}))
.

The domain category plays a crucial role in this construction, so we look to simplify it through the use of final functors, starting with
[
AMGR25
]
Lemma 2.4.5 and Lemma 3.6.5.

Lemma 1.2.7
.

\mathbf{\Delta}^{\sf op}\xrightarrow{(\ref{Chip})}\para^{\sf op}
is a final functor. Therefore the canonical map between spaces

\hh(\mathcal{C}):=\colim\left(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\sf op}\xrightarrow{\chi}{\sf Quiv}^{\sf op}\xrightarrow{\hom_{\sf Cat}(\_,\mathcal{C})}\spaces\right)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\colim\left(\para^{\sf op}\to{\sf Quiv}^{\sf op}\xrightarrow{\hom_{\sf Cat}(\_,\mathcal{C})}\spaces\right)

is an equivalence.

Lemma 1.2.8
.

The map
\para\xrightarrow{(\ref{functor})}\quiv_{/S^{1}}
is final.

Remark 1.2.9
.

These two lemmas imply the canonical map between spaces,

\colim\left(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\sf op}\xrightarrow{\chi}{\sf Quiv}^{\sf op}\xrightarrow{\hom_{\sf Cat}(\_,\mathcal{C})}\spaces\right)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\colim\left(\quiv_{/S^{1}}^{\sf op}\to{\sf Quiv}^{\sf op}\xrightarrow{\hom_{\sf Cat}(\_,\mathcal{C})}\spaces\right)=:\int_{S^{1}}\mathcal{C}~,

is an equivalence.
Here,
\int_{S^{1}}\mathcal{C}
is the factorization homology of
\mathcal{C}
over
S^{1}
. This is Definition 4.1.1 in
[
AMGR25
]
with
M=S^{1}
. The results of this paper are also relevant to those looking to calculate the factorization homology over the circle of Reedy categories due to the following corollary.

Corollary 1.2.10
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category. Then there is a canonical equivalence between spaces

\hh(\mathcal{C})\simeq\int_{S^{1}}\mathcal{C}~.

These lemmas also allow us to replace our domain category with
\para
. The objects of
\para
have a nice representation, as seen in Lemma 2.4.3
[
AMGR25
]
.

Lemma 1.2.11
.

Every object in
\para
is non-canonically isomorphic with
\frac{1}{\ell}\mathbb{Z}
for some
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.

The unstraightening of our functor now has a more explicit form.

Observation 1.2.12
.

For
\mathcal{R}
a Reedy category,

\Un\left(\para^{\sf op}\to{\sf Quiv}^{\sf op}\xrightarrow{\hom_{\sf Cat}(\_,\mathcal{R}_{\leq n})}\spaces\right)
is an ordinary category where an object is a pair
(\lambda,\ell)
with
\lambda\in\para
and
\ell:\overline{\lambda}\to\mathcal{R}_{\leq n}
a functor. A morphism
(\lambda,\ell)\to(\lambda^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})
then is a morphism
\lambda\xrightarrow{f}\lambda^{\prime}
in
\para
and a natural isomorphism:

.

The number and position of the points on the circle are determined by the element in
\para
. Since
\ell
determined the labels of the picture we call it a labeling functor.

By evoking definitions and
Lemma

1.2.5
we see the following.

Observation 1.2.15
.

There is a canonical equivalence between spaces:

(1.2.3)

\displaystyle{\sf HH}(\mathcal{R}_{\leq n})

\displaystyle\hskip 4.30554pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{defhhpara})}}{{\simeq}}\colim\left(\para^{\sf op}\to{\sf Quiv}^{\sf op}\xrightarrow{\hom_{\sf Cat}(\_,\mathcal{R}_{\leq n})}\spaces\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{colimNUn})}}{{\simeq}}\left|\Un\left(\para^{\sf op}\to{\sf Quiv}^{\sf op}\xrightarrow{\hom_{\sf Cat}(\_,\mathcal{R}_{\leq n})}\spaces\right)\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 10.76385pt\overset{Def\ref{defun<n}}{\simeq}|\Un_{\leq n}|~.

1.3.
Loopspace of a Groupoid

We now note a few additional facts, starting with the following theorem.

Proposition 1.3.1
.

Let
G
be a group. There is a canonical equivalence:

\map(S^{1},\mathsf{B}G)\simeq\coprod_{[g]\in G_{/\sf conj}}\mathsf{B}\mathsf{Z}_{G}(g)

Setting
G
set to be
\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r)
gives the following corollary since the stabilizers are given by the centralizers of automorphisms
\phi
, denoted
\mathsf{Z}_{\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r)}(\phi)
.

Corollary 1.3.2
.

For a Reedy category
\mathcal{R}

\hom(S^{1},\mathsf{B}\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r))\simeq\coprod_{[\phi]\in\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r)_{/{\sf conj}}}\mathsf{B}\mathsf{Z}_{\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r)}(\phi)~.

Before proving this theorem we introduce some more definitions and lemmas.

Definition 1.3.3
.

Let
G\curvearrowright X
be a group acting on a set. The
action groupoid,
X_{/\!/G}
, is the groupoid where an object is an element in
X
, and a morphism from
x
to
x^{\prime}
is an element
g\in G
such that
g\cdot x=x^{\prime}
.

Lemma 1.3.4
.

Let
G
and
H
be groups. There is the canonical equivalence between groupoids:

\hom(\mathsf{B}H,\mathsf{B}G)\xleftarrow{\simeq}^{\hskip 3.01389pt\textstyle\hom(H,G)}\hskip-4.30554pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip 3.01389pt\sf conj}}{{\big/\!\big/}}_{\hskip-3.01389pt\textstyle G}~.

Proof.

Consider the functor

{}^{\hskip 3.01389pt\textstyle\hom(H,G)}\hskip-4.30554pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip 3.01389pt\sf conj}}{{\big/\!\big/}}_{\hskip-3.01389pt\textstyle G}\to\hom(\mathsf{B}H,\mathsf{B}G)

given by

f\mapsto\begin{cases}*\mapsto*\\
h\mapsto f(h)\end{cases}

on objects and on morphisms

g\mapsto\eta_{g}:

where
\eta_{g}
is the natural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{B}H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{B}G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{g}}

with
\eta_{g}(*)=g
. This functor is a categorical equivalence. To see this consider the condition for
\eta_{g}
to be a natural transformation: for all
a\in\hom(H,G)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(*)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h(*)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circlearrowright}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(*)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h(*)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

namely that
f(a)=xg(a)x^{-1}

\forall a\in\hom(H,G)
. In other words,
g=x^{-1}fx
which is identically the condition for
x
to be a morphism in
{}^{\hskip 3.01389pt\textstyle\hom(H,G)}\hskip-4.30554pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip 3.01389pt\sf conj}}{{\big/\!\big/}}_{\hskip-3.01389pt\textstyle G}
. Therefore the Hom-sets of the domain and codomain are in bijection, so this functor is fully faithful. Essential surjectivity is clear so they are categorically equivalent.
∎

Corollary 1.3.5
.

For a group
G

\hom(S^{1},\mathsf{B}G)\simeq^{\hskip 3.01389pt\textstyle G}\hskip-4.30554pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip 3.01389pt\sf conj}}{{\big/\!\big/}}_{\hskip-3.01389pt\textstyle G}

Proof.

Since
\mathsf{B}\mathbb{Z}\simeq S^{1}
and
\hom(\mathbb{Z},G)\simeq G
, an application of
Lemma

1.3.4
with
H:=\mathbb{Z}
sees the result.
∎

The following fact about groupoids is the rest of what we need.

Lemma 1.3.6
.

Given a groupoid
\mathcal{G}
, there is an equivalence between groupoids:

\mathcal{G}\simeq\coprod_{[x]\in\pi_{0}\mathcal{G}}\mathsf{B}\Aut_{\mathcal{G}}(x)~.

Proof.

Choose for each
[x]\in\pi_{0}\mathcal{G}
a representative element
x\in[x]
. Consider the map

(1.3.1)

\coprod_{[x]\in\pi_{0}\mathcal{G}}\mathsf{B}\Aut_{\mathcal{G}}(x)\to\mathcal{G}~.

By the universal property of coproducts it is characterized by the functors that select the canonical action
\Aut_{\mathcal{G}}(x)\curvearrowright x
for each
x
:

\mathsf{B}\Aut_{\mathcal{G}}(x)\xrightarrow{\langle\Aut_{\mathcal{G}}(x)\curvearrowright x\rangle}\mathcal{G}~.

This map is clearly essentially surjective. Namely, given an object
g
of
\mathcal{G}
, for
y\in[g]
the chosen representative, then
g\simeq y
.

Additionally this map is fully faithful; note for the left side of
Eq.

1.3.1
:

\hom_{\sf LHS}([x],[y])=\begin{cases}\Aut_{\mathcal{G}}(x)&[x]=[y]\\
\emptyset&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

and the right side

\hom_{\sf RHS}(\Aut_{\mathcal{G}}(x)\curvearrowright x,\Aut_{\mathcal{G}}(y)\curvearrowright y)=\begin{cases}\Aut_{\mathcal{G}}(x)&x\simeq y\\
\emptyset&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}~.

As
\mathcal{G}
is a groupoid,
x\simeq y
if and only if
[x]=[y]
,

\hom_{\sf RHS}(\Aut_{\mathcal{G}}(x)\curvearrowright x,\Aut_{\mathcal{G}}(y)\curvearrowright y)=\hom_{\sf LHS}([x],[y])~.

Therefore this map is an equivalence of categories.
∎

Observation 1.3.7
.

Let
G\curvearrowright X
be a group acting on a set, then
\Aut_{X_{/\!/G}}(x)=G_{x}
where
G_{x}
is the stabilizer subgroup of
x
in G.

This observation and
\pi_{0}\left(X_{/\!/G}\right)=X_{/G}
leads to the following immediate corollary of
Lemma

1.3.6
.

Corollary 1.3.8
.

Given
G
a group and
X
such that
G\curvearrowright X
, then

X_{/\!/G}\simeq\coprod_{[x]\in X_{/G}}\mathsf{B}G_{x}~.

Now we return to the proof of the main result.

Proof of
1.3.1
.

The proof is an application of
Corollary

1.3.5
and
Corollary

1.3.8
. The stabilizers are given by the centralizers since the action is by conjugation. Altogether

\hom(S^{1},\mathsf{B}G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{homotopyquotient})}}{{\simeq}}^{\hskip 3.01389pt\textstyle G}\hskip-4.30554pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip 3.01389pt\sf conj}}{{\big/\!\big/}}_{\hskip-3.01389pt\textstyle G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{cor})}}{{\simeq}}\coprod_{[g]\in G_{/^{\sf conj}G}}\mathsf{B}\mathsf{Z}_{G}(g)~.

∎

2.
Proof of Theorem

\test

Proof.

First
1.1.12
,
1.2.15
,
Corollary

1.2.10
and that colimits commute with colimits implies:

{\sf HH}(\mathcal{R})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{HH=fact}}}{{\simeq}}\int_{S^{1}}\mathcal{R}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{Reedyvaluewisecolim}}}{{\simeq}}\int_{S^{1}}\underset{n\to\infty}{\colim}\mathcal{R}_{\leq n}\\
\simeq\underset{n\to\infty}{\colim}\int_{S^{1}}\mathcal{R}_{\leq n}\quad\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{Unn=HH}}}{{\simeq}}\underset{n\to\infty}{\colim}|\Un_{\leq n}|~.

Therefore we must understand
\underset{n\to\infty}{\colim}|\Un_{\leq n}|
which is understanding
\Un_{\leq n}
by
Lemma

1.1.11
.
We seek out a description of
\Un_{\leq n}
through induction on
n
. Explicitly we aim to show that

|\Un_{\leq n}|\simeq\coprod_{0\geq r\geq}\coprod_{[r]\in\mathcal{R}_{=n}}\coprod_{[\phi]\in\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r)_{/^{\sf conj}}}\mathsf{B}\mathsf{Z}_{\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r)}(\phi)~.

We begin with a base case of
n=0
.
First note that the morphisms in
{\mathcal{R}_{\leq 0}^{-}}
must be isomorphisms since non-isomorphisms lower degree and 0 is the lowest degree. This forces morphisms in
\mathcal{R}_{\leq 0}^{+}
to be isomorphisms since they map between objects of degree 0. Consequently, the Reedy factorization of any morphism
f\in{\mathcal{R}_{\leq 0}}
must be a composition of isomorphisms. Then
\mathcal{R}_{\leq 0}
has only invertible morphisms so letting
[\mathcal{R}_{\leq 0}]^{\sf iso}:=\pi_{0}(\Obj(\mathcal{R}_{\leq 0}))
we see by
Lemma

1.3.6
:

\mathcal{R}_{\leq 0}=\coprod_{[r]\in[\mathcal{R}_{\leq 0}]^{\sf iso}}\mathsf{B}\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r)~.

Thus, nonabelian Poincare duality (
[
AF15
]
Corollary 4.6), and the connectedness of the circle imply

\int_{S^{1}}\mathcal{R}_{\leq 0}\simeq\hom\left(S^{1},\mathcal{R}_{\leq 0}\right)\simeq\hom\left(S^{1},\coprod_{[r]\in[\mathcal{R}_{\leq 0}]^{\sf iso}}\mathsf{B}\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r)\right)\simeq\coprod_{[r]\in[\mathcal{R}_{\leq 0}]^{\sf iso}}\hom(S^{1},\mathsf{B}\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r))~.

Finally recalling
Corollary

1.3.2
gives

\hh(\mathcal{R})\simeq\int_{S^{1}}\mathcal{R}_{\leq 0}\simeq\coprod_{[r]\in[\mathcal{R}_{\leq 0}]^{\sf iso}}\hom(S^{1},\mathsf{B}\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r))\simeq\coprod_{[r]\in[\mathcal{R}_{\leq 0}]^{\sf iso}}\coprod_{[\phi]\in\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r)_{/}{\sf conj}}\mathsf{B}\mathsf{Z}{\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r)}(\phi)~,

as desired.

Now we’re ready to begin the inductive step. First, we show
\Un_{\leq n}\simeq\Un_{\leq n-1}\amalg\Un_{=n}
. Using the factorization of morphisms in a Reedy category and
Lemma

1.2.11
we can consider two endofunctors on
\Un_{\leq n}

\sf sd:{\sf Un}_{\leq n}\longrightarrow\Un_{\leq n}

\sf F:{\sf Un}_{\leq n}\longrightarrow\Un_{\leq n}

indicated pictorially on objects, as in
Remark

1.2.14
, in
Fig.

1
.

(\lambda,\ell)
=
T_{(\lambda,\ell)}
X_{0}
X_{L}
X_{1}
f_{L}
f_{0}
f_{L-1}
f_{1}
T_{(\lambda,\ell)}
\sf sd(\lambda,\ell)
=
\im(f_{0})
X_{0}
\im(f_{L})
X_{L}
X_{1}
f^{-}_{0}
f^{+}_{0}
f^{-}_{L}
f^{+}_{1}
f^{+}_{L}
f^{-}_{1}
S_{(\lambda,\ell)}
F(\lambda,\ell)
=
S_{(\lambda,\ell)}
\im(f_{0})
\im(f_{L})
f^{+}_{0}\circ f^{-}_{0}
f^{+}_{L}\circ f^{-}_{L}
f^{+}_{1}\circ f^{-}_{1}

Figure 1
.
Pictorial definition on objects of two functors and two natural inclusions

We now give an explicit construction of these functors. We begin by recalling
Lemma

1.2.11
: that the full subcategory of
\para
consisting of objects of the form
\frac{1}{L}\mathbb{Z}
is a skeleton. Thus any functor defined on
\Un_{\leq n}
need only be specified on elements of the form
\left(\frac{1}{L}\mathbb{Z},\overline{\frac{1}{L}\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{\ell}\mathcal{R}_{\leq n}\right)
. Recall
\overline{\frac{1}{L}\mathbb{Z}}
is the quiver with objects
\{\frac{k}{L}+\mathbb{Z}\}_{0\leq k<L}
and morphisms freely generated by
\frac{k}{L}+\mathbb{Z}\to\frac{k+1}{L}+\mathbb{Z}
for each
0\leq k<L
. Then
\ell
is specified by for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{/L\mathbb{Z}}
an object of
\mathcal{R}
with degree less than n, say
X_{k}:=\ell(\frac{k}{L}+\mathbb{Z})
, and a morphism
f_{k}:X_{k}\to X_{k+1}
. Now to define
\sf sd
:

\sd\left(\frac{k}{L}\mathbb{Z},\ell\right):=\left(\frac{k}{2L}\mathbb{Z},\overline{\frac{k}{2L}\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{\ell_{\sd}}\mathcal{R}_{\leq n}\right)

where
\ell_{\sd}
is defined on objects by for
p\in\mathbb{Z}_{/2L\mathbb{Z}}

\ell_{\sd}\left(\frac{p}{2L}+\mathbb{Z}\right):=\begin{cases}X_{p/2}&\text{p even}\\
{\sf im}\left(f_{\frac{p-1}{2}}\right)&\text{p odd}~,\end{cases}

and, with a slight abuse of notation, on morphisms by

\ell_{\sd}\left(\frac{p}{2L}+\mathbb{Z}\to\frac{p+1}{2L}+\mathbb{Z}\right):=\begin{cases}f^{-}_{p/2}:X_{p/2}\to{\sf im}(f_{p/2})&\text{p even}\\
f^{+}_{\frac{p+1}{2}}:{\sf im}\left(f_{\frac{p-1}{2}}\right)\to X_{\frac{p+1}{2}}&\text{p odd}~,\end{cases}

where the indices are taken modulo
L
.
Similarly we can define a map
F
by

F\left(\frac{k}{L}\mathbb{Z},\ell\right):=\left(\frac{k}{L}\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2L},\overline{\frac{2k+1}{2L}\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{\ell_{F}}\mathcal{R}_{\leq n}\right)

where
\ell_{F}
is defined on objects by

\ell_{F}\left(\frac{k}{L}+\mathbb{Z}\right):=\sf{im}(f_{k})~,

and on morphisms by

\ell_{F}\left(\frac{k}{L}+\mathbb{Z}\to\frac{k+1}{L}+\mathbb{Z}\right):=f^{+}_{k+1}\circ f^{-}_{k+1}~.

We now describe where these functors take morphisms. Towards this, consider
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\para
where
\lambda
can be represented by
\frac{1}{L}\mathbb{Z}
and
\lambda^{\prime}
by
\frac{1}{M}\mathbb{Z}
. Additionally define
\ell:\overline{\lambda}\to\mathcal{R}_{\leq n}
and
\ell^{\prime}:\overline{\lambda^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{R}_{\leq n}
on both objects and morphisms by:

\displaystyle\ell_{\sf obj}:\frac{k}{L}+\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\mapsto X_{k}

\displaystyle\ell_{\sf mor}:\left(\frac{k}{L}+\mathbb{Z}\to\frac{k+1}{L}+\mathbb{Z}\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left[f_{k}:X_{k}\to X_{k+1}\right]

and

\displaystyle\ell^{\prime}_{\sf obj}:\frac{j}{M}+\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\mapsto Y_{j}

\displaystyle\ell^{\prime}_{\sf mor}:\left(\frac{j}{M}+\mathbb{Z}\to\frac{j+1}{M}+\mathbb{Z}\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left[h_{j}:Y_{j}\to Y_{j+1}\right]

respectively.
For all morphisms
g:\lambda\to\lambda^{\prime}
in
\para
the functor
\sd
is defined as follows:

Here,
\sd(g)
is defined via:

\sd(g):\normalsize{\frac{k}{2L}\mapsto}\begin{cases}g(\frac{k/2}{L})&\text{k even}\\
g(\frac{(k-1)/2}{L})+\frac{1}{2M}&\text{k odd}\end{cases}~.

Note
{\sf sd}(g)
does take values in
\overline{\sd(\lambda^{\prime})}\simeq\frac{1}{2M}\mathbb{Z}
as desired. For ease of notation, we write
g(k):=p
instead of
g\left(\frac{k}{L}\right)=\frac{p}{M}
.

The natural transformation,
{\sf sd}(\eta)
, extends
\eta
. Namely
\eta
necessitates the existence of the following commutative diagram for all
k
and for all morphisms
q
in
\para

Therefore, by
Lemma

1.1.10
the necessary map exists and there are factorizations as in
Eq.

2.0.2
and
Eq.

2.0.3
. Then
{\sf sd}(\eta)
is indeed a natural transformation in
Eq.

2.0.1
. So
\sd
is a functor.

This process can be repeated for
F
, in fact it is significantly simpler:

F(g):\frac{2k+1}{2L}\mapsto g\left(\frac{k}{L}\right)+\frac{1}{2M}~.

Finally, we look to define the natural inclusions as natural transformations. These can be defined abstractly by recalling
\Un_{\leq n}\to\para
is a Cartesian fibration, so there exists final fillers

Denote these fillers by
\langle T\rangle
and respectively
\langle S\rangle
. More explicitly T is:

T:\id\to\sd\\

on objects and

T_{\left(\frac{1}{L}\mathbb{Z},\ell\right)}:\left(\frac{1}{L}\mathbb{Z},\ell\right)\to\sd\left(\frac{1}{L}\mathbb{Z},\ell\right)~.

on morphisms.
This is a morphism in
\Un_{\leq n}
and therefore can be specified by a morphism in
\para
and a communtative triangle. Then
T
is given by

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle:\frac{1}{L}\mathbb{Z}\to\frac{1}{2L}\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle:\frac{k}{L}\mapsto\frac{2k}{2L},

and

Then
|F|
and
|\sd|
are homotopic to the identity and so is
|F^{r}|
for
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. We have now reduced showing
|\Un_{\leq n}|\simeq|\Un_{\leq n-1}|\amalg|U|
for some
U
, to
|F^{r}(\Un_{\leq n})|\simeq|\Un_{\leq n-1}|\amalg|U|

Before we can show
F^{r}
does take values in the desired disjoint union, we consider the full subcategory
(\para^{\op})_{\leq N}\subset\para^{\op}
with elements isomorphic to
\frac{1}{L}\mathbb{Z}
where
L\leq N
, i.e.
\lambda
such that the quiver
\overline{\lambda}
has at most
N
objects.
Then
\underset{N\to\infty}{\colim}(\para^{\op})_{\leq N}=\para^{\op}
so

\displaystyle\Un_{\leq n}

\displaystyle\simeq\Un\left(\underset{N\to\infty}{\colim}[(\para^{\op})_{\leq N}]\to{\sf Quiv}^{\sf op}\xrightarrow{\hom_{\sf Cat}(\_,\mathcal{R}_{\leq n})}\spaces\right)

(2.0.4)

\displaystyle\simeq\underset{N\to\infty}{\colim}\left[\Un\left((\para^{\op})_{\leq N}\to{\sf Quiv}^{\sf op}\xrightarrow{\hom_{\sf Cat}(\_,\mathcal{R}_{\leq n})}\spaces\right)\right]

where
Eq.

2.0.4
is due to Cartesian fibrations being exponentiable fibrations as shown in Lemma 2.15 in
[
AF20
]
. We will use the notation

\Un_{\leq n,\leq N}:=\left[\Un\left((\para^{\op})_{\leq N}\to{\sf Quiv}^{\sf op}\xrightarrow{\hom_{\sf Cat}(\_,\mathcal{R}_{\leq n})}\spaces\right)\right]~.

Now we induct on N to show
F^{k}
takes values in
\Un\left(\para^{\sf op}\to{\sf Quiv}^{\sf op}\xrightarrow{\hom_{\sf Cat}(\_,\mathcal{R}_{=n})}\spaces\right)\amalg\Un_{\leq n-1}
for some large enough
k
. For the sake of readability we denote

\Un_{=n}:=\Un\left(\para^{\sf op}\to{\sf Quiv}^{\sf op}\xrightarrow{\hom_{\sf Cat}(\_,\mathsf{B}\mathcal{R}_{=n})}\spaces\right)~.

Note that for
(\lambda,\ell)\in\Un_{=n}
, then
\ell
either takes objects to objects of degree strictly less than
n
or morphisms to isomorphisms

First the base case: let
(\lambda,\ell)\in\Un_{\leq n,\leq 1}
. The unique vertex is labelled as an object with degree
n
or with degree less than
n
. If the degree is less then
n
, than
(\lambda,\ell)\in\Un_{\leq n-1}
so we are done. If the degree is equal to
n
, either the labeled morphism is an isomorphism, in which case
(\lambda,\ell)\in\Un_{=n}
, or the labelled morphism is not an isomorphism in which case the degree of the image of the morphism is less than
n
so
F(\lambda,\ell)\in\Un_{\leq n-1}
.

Now we proceed to the induction step. Let
(\lambda,\ell)\in\Un_{\leq n,\leq N}
. Let
L\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
\lambda\simeq\frac{1}{L}\mathbb{Z}\in\para
. Denote
X_{n}:=\ell\left(\frac{n}{L}+\mathbb{Z}\right)
and
f_{n}:=\ell\left(\frac{n}{L}+\mathbb{Z}\to\frac{n+1}{L}+\mathbb{Z}\right)
. Note, if
L<N
we’re done by induction and if
L=1
we are done by the base case, so suppose
L=N>1
.
Now suppose there exists
i
such that
|{\sf im}(f_{i})|\leq n
. Without loss of generality let
i=1
. Then both
\deg({\sf im}(f^{+}_{1}\circ f^{-}_{1}))
and
\deg({\sf im}(f^{+}_{2}\circ f^{-}_{2}))
must be less than
n
, by
Lemma

1.1.9
. This application of
Lemma

1.1.9
is depicted by
Fig.

2
. Each application of
F
causes one additional vertex to be mapped to an object with degree less than
n
. This process can be repeated until all of the vertices are mapped to objects with degree less than
n
. Explicitly,

\hbox to291.59pt{\vbox to95.25pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 141.37044pt\lower-46.51389pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \par{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

Figure 2
.
Given that
X_{2}
is degree less than
n
, then both
{\sf im}(f)
and
{\sf im}(g)
must also have degree less than
n
as
f^{+}
is increasing and
f^{-}
is decreasing. Here the blue denotes that the degree of the object is less than
n
.

Therefore by repeating
F
a maximum of
N
times, all vertices are carried to objects with degree less then
n
. Altogether we see that the image of
F^{N}
bifurcates: either
\ell
carries all morphisms of
\lambda
to isomorphisms, in which case
F^{N}(\lambda,\ell)=(\lambda,\ell)
and
F^{N-1}(\lambda,\ell)\in\Un\left((\para^{\sf op})_{=N}\to{\sf Quiv}^{\sf op}\xrightarrow{\hom_{\sf Cat}(\_,\mathcal{R}_{=n})}\spaces\right)
, or
F^{N}(\lambda,\ell)\in\Un_{\leq n-1,\leq N}
. We conclude that

|\Un_{\leq n-1,\leq N}|\simeq\left|\Un\left((\para^{\sf op})_{=N}\to{\sf Quiv}^{\sf op}\xrightarrow{\hom_{\sf Cat}(\_,\mathcal{R}_{=n})}\spaces\right)\right|\amalg\left|\Un_{\leq n-1,\leq N}\right|~.

This fact and manipulations of colimits gives

\displaystyle|\Un_{\leq n}|

\displaystyle\simeq\left|\underset{N\to\infty}{\colim}\Un_{\leq n,\leq N}\right|

(2.0.6)

\displaystyle\simeq\underset{N\to\infty}{\colim}\left|\Un\left((\para^{\sf op})_{=N}\to{\sf Quiv}^{\sf op}\xrightarrow{\hom_{\sf Cat}(\_,\mathsf{B}\mathcal{R}_{=n})}\spaces\right)\right|\amalg\left|\Un_{\leq n-1,\leq N}\right|

(2.0.7)

\displaystyle\simeq\left|\underset{N\to\infty}{\colim}\left[\Un\left((\para^{\sf op})_{=N}\to{\sf Quiv}^{\sf op}\xrightarrow{\hom_{\sf Cat}(\_,\mathsf{B}\mathcal{R}_{=n})}\spaces\right)\right]\right|\amalg\left|\Un_{\leq n-1}\right|

(2.0.8)

\displaystyle\simeq\left|\Un\left(\underset{N\to\infty}{\colim}\left[(\para^{\sf op})_{=N}\right]\to{\sf Quiv}^{\sf op}\xrightarrow{\hom_{\sf Cat}(\_,\mathsf{B}\mathcal{R}_{=n})}\spaces\right)\right|\amalg|\Un_{\leq n-1}|

\displaystyle\simeq\left|\Un\left(\para^{\sf op}\to{\sf Quiv}^{\sf op}\xrightarrow{\hom_{\sf Cat}(\_,\mathsf{B}\mathcal{R}_{=n})}\spaces\right)\right|\amalg\left|\Un_{\leq n-1}\right|

(2.0.9)

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle=|\Un_{=n}|\amalg|\Un_{\leq n-1}|~.

Here
Eq.

2.0.6
is due to the above discussion;
Eq.

2.0.7
is exploiting the commutativity of the geometric realization and disjoint union and applying
Section

2
;
Eq.

2.0.8
is again because cartesian fibrations are exponentiable. This equivalence along with our inductive hypothesis gives

\displaystyle{\sf HH}(\mathcal{R}_{\leq n})

\displaystyle\simeq\int_{S^{1}}\mathcal{R}_{\leq n}

\displaystyle\simeq|\Un_{\leq n}|

(2.0.10)

\displaystyle\simeq\left|\Un_{=n}\right|\amalg\left|\Un_{\leq n-1}\right|

(2.0.11)

\displaystyle\simeq\left|\Un_{=n}\right|\amalg{\sf HH}(\mathcal{R}_{\leq n-1})

(2.0.12)

\displaystyle\simeq\left|\Un_{=n}\right|\amalg\left[\coprod_{0\leq q\leq n-1}\coprod_{[r]\in\mathcal{R}_{=(q-1)}}\coprod_{[\phi]\in\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r)_{/^{\sf conj}}}\mathsf{B}\mathsf{Z}_{\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r)}(\phi)\right].

Here
Eq.

2.0.10
is due to
Eq.

2.0.9
;
Eq.

2.0.11
uses
1.2.12
;
Eq.

2.0.12
is utilizing the inductive hypothesis.
To finally finish our inductive argument, we need to show
|\Un_{=n}|
is isomorphic with

\coprod_{[r]\in\mathcal{R}_{=n}}\coprod_{[\phi]\in\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r)_{/^{\sf conj}}}\mathsf{B}\mathsf{Z}_{\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r)}(\phi)~.

Towards this, we note
\left|\Un_{=n}\right|\simeq\int_{S^{1}}\mathsf{B}\mathcal{R}_{=n}\simeq\int_{S^{1}}^{\alpha}\mathcal{R}_{=n}
as shown in
[
AMGR25
]
. Then by non-abelian Poincaré Duality
\int_{S^{1}}^{\alpha}\mathcal{R}_{=n}\simeq\map(S^{1},\mathsf{B}\mathcal{R}_{=n})
. Since
\mathcal{R}_{=n}
is a groupoid, the circle is connected, and
1.3.1
:

\displaystyle\map(S^{1},\mathsf{B}\mathcal{R}_{=n})

\displaystyle\simeq\map(S^{1},\coprod_{[r]\in\mathcal{R}_{=n}}\mathsf{B}\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r))

\displaystyle\simeq\coprod_{[r]\in\mathcal{R}_{=n}}\map(S^{1},\mathsf{B}\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r))

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{MapS1})}{\simeq}\coprod_{[r]\in\mathcal{R}_{=n}}\coprod_{[\phi]\in\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r)_{/^{\sf conj}}}\mathsf{B}\mathsf{Z}_{\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r)}(\phi)~.

All of these facts together give an equivalence:

\left|\Un_{=n}\right|\simeq\coprod_{[r]\in\mathcal{R}_{=n}}\coprod_{[\phi]\in\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r)_{/^{\sf conj}}}\mathsf{B}\mathsf{Z}_{\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r)}(\phi)~.

An application of
Eq.

2.0.12
leads to the desired result to complete the induction:

\displaystyle{\sf HH}(\mathcal{R}_{\leq n})

\displaystyle\simeq\underset{0\geq r\geq n}{\coprod}\hskip 4.30554pt\coprod_{[r]\in\mathcal{R}_{=n}}\coprod_{[\phi]\in\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r)_{/^{\sf conj}}}\mathsf{B}\mathsf{Z}_{\Aut_{\mathcal{R}}(r)}(\phi)~.

∎
